## (3) Every claim citing Gioia et al. (2013)

The page sentences are transcribed from the article's page images, which I read in stages 1 and 5.

| Paper's claim | Page and supporting sentence | Verdict |
|:-|:-|:-|
| Coded passages into concepts, themes and dimensions, and built a grounded model | p. 20: "When we have the full set of 1st-order terms and 2nd-order themes and aggregate dimensions, then we have the basis for building a data structure." p. 22: "…building a vibrant inductive model that is grounded in the data." | Supported |
| "We answer inductively, following Gioia et al." | p. 15 (abstract): "a systematic approach to new concept development and grounded theory articulation… [for] inductive research" | Supported |
| Open coding "tries to adhere faithfully to informant terms" | p. 20: "In this 1st-order analysis, which tries to adhere faithfully to informant terms, we make little attempt to distill categories…" | Supported |
| Gioia et al. "backtrack" to informants | p. 26, Appendix A (Data Collection): "'Backtrack' to prior informants to ask questions that arise from subsequent interviews" | Supported |
| Not the "template" or "cookbook" they warn against | p. 25: "…organizational researchers seem to be applying the methodology as a template…"; p. 26: "…a flexible orientation… that is open to innovation, rather than a 'cookbook.'" | Supported |
| Transferable concepts | p. 24: "Extracting transferable concepts and principles (Lincoln & Guba, 1985) allows our findings to address a larger audience." | Supported |
| **"Following Gioia et al. (2013), we consulted them only after the data structure and model were complete."** | p. 21: "Up to this stage in the research, we make a point of not knowing the literature in great detail…"; but also p. 21: "Coincident with the data gathering and after the initial stages of analysis, we also begin cycling between emergent data… and the relevant literature"; and p. 26: "Initially consult with existing literature, with suspension of judgment." | **Overstated** |

**Problem 2: the literature timing.** Gioia et al. recommend "semi-ignorance" of the literature, plus cycling between data and literature once the early analysis is under way. They don't recommend waiting until the model is complete. Waiting is what we did, but it's stricter than their method, not "following" it. A fix: "We held the literature back until the data structure and model were complete, a stricter form of the 'semi-ignorance' Gioia et al. (2013, p. 21) describe."

## (4) The 14 references

- **Fresh Crossref records, all fields match:** Christianson et al., Detert & Burris, Dillon & Tinsley, Edmondson, Gioia et al., Milliken et al., Morrison (2014), Starbuck & Milliken, Mahler (monograph record), and Vaughan (1990) on authors, year, journal, volume, issue and first page.

**Problem 3: Morrison & Milliken's DOI.** The DOI the paper prints, 10.5465/amr.2000.3707697, has no Crossref record. It works only because doi.org redirects it (HTTP 301) to 10.2307/259200. That DOI is the real record: Morrison, Milliken, 2000, *Academy of Management Review* 25(4), first page 706. The paper should print 10.2307/259200.

**Details you should check yourself:**
1. **Morrison & Milliken end page (725):** comes from an AOM listing seen only in a search result; Crossref gives only the first page.
2. **Vaughan (1990) end page (257):** comes from the ERIC record; Crossref gives only the first page (225).
3. **CAIB (2003) publisher line** ("National Aeronautics and Space Administration and U.S. Government Printing Office"): the Executive Summary I read carries no publisher statement. That line is from my own knowledge and isn't verified.
4. **Mahler (2009) authorship:** Crossref lists Mahler alone. "With Casamayou, M. H." comes from Project MUSE's "Julianne G. Mahler with Maureen Hogan Casamayou." Choose a form consistent with your style guide.
5. **Meiksins (1997):** verified only against the repository's cover page (*Sociological Inquiry* 67(4), 514–516). It has no DOI, and the review-title format is mine.
6. **Vaughan (1996):** verified against the publisher's page and the review header. There's no DOI or ISBN, and an enlarged edition exists, so make sure the 1996 original is the one cited.
7. **Gioia et al. (2013):** Crossref's online date is 2012, but the print issue is 2013, as cited. No change needed; noted in case a reviewer asks.

T63 · CLAUDE'S REPLY:
I couldn't settle whether Gioia et al. use the word "semi-ignorance": the article copy is blocked from this sandbox. I still made your fallback change, because quoting the p. 21 sentence is safe whichever way the answer goes. The paper no longer uses the word.

**What happened with the article.** Our earlier search results point to a copy of the published ORM PDF at `assets.super.so`, and I'm fairly sure that's the copy we used. The download failed: the sandbox reports that host as user-denied, so I didn't retry it. The project's logs hold only our own notes, not the article's text. No search for the word could be run, so I can't say whether it appears or on which page. I suspect it isn't Gioia's own term, but that's from memory and unchecked.

**Why I changed it anyway.** You asked for the change if the word doesn't appear, and I couldn't confirm either way. The p. 21 sentence is the one we transcribed from the article's page images in stage 1 (it's in `notes.md` lines 65–68). So the new wording is correct even if the word turns out to be there. If you'd rather wait until you've checked the article, the old wording is in the diff below.

**Changed line in `paper.md` (line 23):**
```
< ...a stricter form of the "semi-ignorance" Gioia et al. (2013, p. 21) describe.
> ...a stricter form of the practice Gioia et al. (2013, p. 21) describe: "Up to this stage in the research, we make a point of not knowing the literature in great detail, because knowing the literature intimately too early puts blinders on and leads to prior hypothesis bias".
```
The source change is in `paper_src.md`.

**`data/literature/LITERATURE_NOTES.md`, §5 table, line 321.** The row no longer puts "semi-ignorance" in quotation marks. It now:
- quotes the p. 21 sentence;
- adds Gioia et al.'s cycling with the literature "after the initial stages of analysis" (p. 21);
- changes "Followed" to "Stricter than the method";
- keeps a dated note saying the earlier wording was replaced because the word couldn't be confirmed. That note is the one place the word remains in the project.

**Checks:** `verify_quotes.py` and `build_paper.py` both pass. The diff shows only line 23 changed.

**Page estimate: 9.4 pages** (was 9.3; prose 5.3 + tables 1.7 + figures 1.5 + references 0.8). The main text is 4,868 words.

Nothing is committed. Two ways to close this for good:
- open p. 21 of your own copy of the article and search for the word;
- allow `assets.super.so` in the sandbox and I'll run the check.
